People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello doctor. I suppose what made me finally come in is that my eating has gotten all... well, it's just all turned around lately. Like, some days I just forget lunch completely. I'll be sitting there looking out the window—my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she planted these new hydrangeas and they are just not taking to the soil at all. I told her she needs more acidity, maybe some coffee grounds, but she never listens to me. 

Anyway, where was I? Oh, the eating. Yes, I just forget to have lunch. And then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter eating handfuls of crackers and sweets. Though honestly, nothing even tastes right anymore. The crackers are those saltines in the blue box, you know? They used to be 99 cents and now they're nearly three dollars at the grocery store. It's just ridiculous what things cost now. But yes, my eating is just all out of sorts.

Well, sad... yes, I suppose that's one way to put it. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, low feeling. It's just like the color has completely gone out of the day before I even manage to get dressed.

Well, focus... yes, it's just been dreadful lately. I'll sit there staring at the simplest things, like trying to decide whether to call the pharmacy for my refills or open the mail first, and my mind just gets so terribly foggy that I end up doing neither. 

Speaking of the mail, my postman, Arthur, he's been leaving my letters half hanging out of the box again. I've told him a hundred times that if it rains, everything gets soaked. Last month, my electric bill was completely ruined. The ink ran everywhere, just a big blue smudge across the paper. I had to call the company, and you know how that goes—press one for this, press two for that. I was on hold for nearly forty-five minutes listening to that awful elevator music. It sounded like someone playing a flute underwater. They finally put me through to a young man who spoke so fast I could barely catch a word. I think they train them to talk like that so you'll just give up and hang up the phone. 

But... what were we talking about? Oh, right, making decisions. Yes, I just sit there and the fog rolls in, and I can't seem to figure out what to do first.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, moving slowly... yes, I suppose I have been. My body just feels so bogged down lately. It's like it takes me twice as long just to cross the living room or... or button up my sweater. 

Speaking of sweaters, I was trying to button my blue cardigan the other day, the one with the pearl buttons. I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. 

But sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I'll just pace around the house, walking from the kitchen to the hall and back, carrying the same stack of papers in my hand. I don't even know what I'm looking for half the time. 

Anyway... what was the question? Oh, yes, moving slowly. Yes, it takes me ages to do the simplest things, except for when I'm pacing around with those papers.

Well, when it started... let me think. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. 

I remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. 

Anyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.

Yes, it's just a very heavy feeling in the mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or felt unusually fatigued lately, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Well, fatigue... no, I haven't really noticed being unusually tired. I mean, I sleep fine, mostly. I have that nice down comforter I got from QVC. Oh, the woman who sold it on there, she had the most soothing voice. I used to watch that channel just to have some company in the house, you know? They sell the most interesting things. Last week they had this gadget that chops garlic without getting your hands smelly. Because you know, once you get garlic on your fingers, it just stays there for days. You can wash and wash with dish soap, even that lemon-scented kind, but it doesn't do a bit of good. My mother always said to rub your hands on a stainless steel spoon under cold water, but who has the time to stand at the sink doing that? \n\nAnyway, what was it you said? Oh, energy. No, my energy seems about the same as it usually is for someone my age.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or concentrate on tasks lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Well, focus... yes, it's just been dreadful lately. I'll sit there staring at the simplest things, like trying to decide whether to call the pharmacy for my refills or open the mail first, and my mind just gets so terribly foggy that I end up doing neither. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, my postman, Arthur, he's been leaving my letters half hanging out of the box again. I've told him a hundred times that if it rains, everything gets soaked. Last month, my electric bill was completely ruined. The ink ran everywhere, just a big blue smudge across the paper. I had to call the company, and you know how that goes—press one for this, press two for that. I was on hold for nearly forty-five minutes listening to that awful elevator music. It sounded like someone playing a flute underwater. They finally put me through to a young man who spoke so fast I could barely catch a word. I think they train them to talk like that so you'll just give up and hang up the phone. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, making decisions. Yes, I just sit there and the fog rolls in, and I can't seem to figure out what to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Well, moving slowly... yes, I suppose I have been. My body just feels so bogged down lately. It's like it takes me twice as long just to cross the living room or... or button up my sweater. \n\nSpeaking of sweaters, I was trying to button my blue cardigan the other day, the one with the pearl buttons. I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. \n\nBut sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I'll just pace around the house, walking from the kitchen to the hall and back, carrying the same stack of papers in my hand. I don't even know what I'm looking for half the time. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, yes, moving slowly. Yes, it takes me ages to do the simplest things, except for when I'm pacing around with those papers.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — can you pinpoint when these feelings of heaviness and difficulty with daily tasks started?\n\nPatient: Well, when it started... let me think. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. \n\nI remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. \n\nAnyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. \n\nI remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. \n\nAnyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, or have you been concerned about your appearance or body image?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around, where some days I forget lunch completely and other days I stand at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my weight... yes, things have definitely changed. What really made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around lately. Some days I'll just completely forget to eat lunch. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. 

Speaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone just wears those elastic belts that stretch out after a month. I bought one for my nephew a few years ago for his birthday, and it was practically useless by Thanksgiving. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. 

But... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. Yes, it's just all turned around. Forgetting lunch, eating crackers... nothing tastes right anymore.

I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. \n\nBut sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. \n\nSpeaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, activities... yes, I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to things. I had my watercolor class, the quilting circle, and my Thursday book club. But lately, I'll pack my bag, leave it right there by the front door, and I just... I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out the door. 

I used to carry this lovely canvas tote bag to book club. It had a print of Monet's water lilies on it. I bought it at the museum gift shop when they had that Impressionist exhibit back in... oh, it must have been 2015. The line to get in went all the way around the block. We had to stand outside in the drizzle for nearly an hour. The woman in front of me had this bright yellow umbrella that kept dripping right onto my shoulder. I didn't want to make a fuss, but it was quite annoying. I've never cared much for the color yellow anyway. It washes me out terribly. My mother always told me I was a "winter" and should stick to jewel tones. She had this stunning emerald green velvet dress she wore to holiday parties. Velvet is so difficult to care for, though. You can't just toss it in the wash. You have to take it to the dry cleaners, and they use all those harsh chemicals. I read an article in the paper the other day about how those chemicals are getting into the groundwater and affecting the local frogs. It's a shame, really. I used to hear frogs singing every spring from the little creek behind my childhood home. 

Anyway... what was the question? Oh, losing interest. Yes, I just leave my bag by the door now. I just can't bring myself to go to my classes or book club anymore.

But yes, my eating is just all out of sorts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, sad... yes, I suppose that's one way to put it. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, low feeling. It's just like the color has completely gone out of the day before I even manage to get dressed. \n\nSpeaking of getting dressed, I was looking in my closet on Tuesday and realized I haven't worn my yellow cardigan in ages. I bought it at Macy's back when they actually had decent sales and real customer service. There was this lovely woman, Margaret, in the misses department. She always knew exactly what size I needed without even asking. She retired, oh, must be five or six years ago now. Moved down to Florida to live with her oldest daughter, near Tampa, I think. I hear the humidity down there is just terrible for your hair, not to mention the bugs. Palmetto bugs, they call them, but let's be honest, they're really just giant roaches. I could never live with that, I'd be terrified to open my own kitchen cabinets. \n\nBut... what were you asking? Oh, right, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy feeling in the mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or felt unusually fatigued lately, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Well, fatigue... no, I haven't really noticed being unusually tired. I mean, I sleep fine, mostly. I have that nice down comforter I got from QVC. Oh, the woman who sold it on there, she had the most soothing voice. I used to watch that channel just to have some company in the house, you know? They sell the most interesting things. Last week they had this gadget that chops garlic without getting your hands smelly. Because you know, once you get garlic on your fingers, it just stays there for days. You can wash and wash with dish soap, even that lemon-scented kind, but it doesn't do a bit of good. My mother always said to rub your hands on a stainless steel spoon under cold water, but who has the time to stand at the sink doing that? \n\nAnyway, what was it you said? Oh, energy. No, my energy seems about the same as it usually is for someone my age.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or concentrate on tasks lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Well, focus... yes, it's just been dreadful lately. I'll sit there staring at the simplest things, like trying to decide whether to call the pharmacy for my refills or open the mail first, and my mind just gets so terribly foggy that I end up doing neither. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, my postman, Arthur, he's been leaving my letters half hanging out of the box again. I've told him a hundred times that if it rains, everything gets soaked. Last month, my electric bill was completely ruined. The ink ran everywhere, just a big blue smudge across the paper. I had to call the company, and you know how that goes—press one for this, press two for that. I was on hold for nearly forty-five minutes listening to that awful elevator music. It sounded like someone playing a flute underwater. They finally put me through to a young man who spoke so fast I could barely catch a word. I think they train them to talk like that so you'll just give up and hang up the phone. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, making decisions. Yes, I just sit there and the fog rolls in, and I can't seem to figure out what to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Well, moving slowly... yes, I suppose I have been. My body just feels so bogged down lately. It's like it takes me twice as long just to cross the living room or... or button up my sweater. \n\nSpeaking of sweaters, I was trying to button my blue cardigan the other day, the one with the pearl buttons. I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. \n\nBut sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I'll just pace around the house, walking from the kitchen to the hall and back, carrying the same stack of papers in my hand. I don't even know what I'm looking for half the time. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, yes, moving slowly. Yes, it takes me ages to do the simplest things, except for when I'm pacing around with those papers.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — can you pinpoint when these feelings of heaviness and difficulty with daily tasks started?\n\nPatient: Well, when it started... let me think. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. \n\nI remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. \n\nAnyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, or have you been concerned about your appearance or body image?\n\nPatient: Well, my weight... yes, things have definitely changed. What really made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around lately. Some days I'll just completely forget to eat lunch. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. \n\nSpeaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone just wears those elastic belts that stretch out after a month. I bought one for my nephew a few years ago for his birthday, and it was practically useless by Thanksgiving. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. Yes, it's just all turned around. Forgetting lunch, eating crackers... nothing tastes right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, activities... yes, I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to things. I had my watercolor class, the quilting circle, and my Thursday book club. But lately, I'll pack my bag, leave it right there by the front door, and I just... I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out the door. \n\nI used to carry this lovely canvas tote bag to book club. It had a print of Monet's water lilies on it. I bought it at the museum gift shop when they had that Impressionist exhibit back in... oh, it must have been 2015. The line to get in went all the way around the block. We had to stand outside in the drizzle for nearly an hour. The woman in front of me had this bright yellow umbrella that kept dripping right onto my shoulder. I didn't want to make a fuss, but it was quite annoying. I've never cared much for the color yellow anyway. It washes me out terribly. My mother always told me I was a \"winter\" and should stick to jewel tones. She had this stunning emerald green velvet dress she wore to holiday parties. Velvet is so difficult to care for, though. You can't just toss it in the wash. You have to take it to the dry cleaners, and they use all those harsh chemicals. I read an article in the paper the other day about how those chemicals are getting into the groundwater and affecting the local frogs. It's a shame, really. I used to hear frogs singing every spring from the little creek behind my childhood home. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, losing interest. Yes, I just leave my bag by the door now. I just can't bring myself to go to my classes or book club anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Guilty? Oh, I don't know about that. I don't really think I've been blaming myself for things, no. I mean, I try to just let things be. 

Like the other day, I was at the grocery store, trying to find those little tins of anchovies. My father used to love anchovies on his pizza. He’d order a whole pie just for himself with extra cheese and those little salty fish. We used to get them from this Italian place down on Main Street—Luigi's, I think it was called. They had those red and white checkered tablecloths that always felt a bit sticky, no matter how much they wiped them down. I always wondered what they used to clean those tables. Probably some cheap industrial cleaner. You have to be so careful with cleaning products these days. I bought a new bottle of window cleaner last month, and the smell was just overpowering. It smelled like artificial pine needles, but not the nice kind you smell in the forest. I went to a cabin in the mountains once, up near Asheville, and the pine trees there were just magnificent. The air was so crisp. We saw a black bear wandering right near the edge of the woods. It didn't bother us, just sniffed around a fallen log and ambled away. They say you're supposed to make a lot of noise if you see a bear, but I just froze. I couldn't have made a sound if I tried. 

But... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling guilty. No, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or blaming myself for things.

the record worries were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had eased enough that I was eating and getting dressed more normally, but I still felt sure the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped teaching my craft classes and have missed calls, appointments, and family tasks I used to handle carefully. I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely more on reminders and put off things that used to be routine.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, since my sister’s health took that serious turn.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The ideas about hidden messages in the family and medical papers started about a year and a month ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, or have you been concerned about your appearance or body image?\n\nPatient: Well, my weight... yes, things have definitely changed. What really made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around lately. Some days I'll just completely forget to eat lunch. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. \n\nSpeaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone just wears those elastic belts that stretch out after a month. I bought one for my nephew a few years ago for his birthday, and it was practically useless by Thanksgiving. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. Yes, it's just all turned around. Forgetting lunch, eating crackers... nothing tastes right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, activities... yes, I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to things. I had my watercolor class, the quilting circle, and my Thursday book club. But lately, I'll pack my bag, leave it right there by the front door, and I just... I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out the door. \n\nI used to carry this lovely canvas tote bag to book club. It had a print of Monet's water lilies on it. I bought it at the museum gift shop when they had that Impressionist exhibit back in... oh, it must have been 2015. The line to get in went all the way around the block. We had to stand outside in the drizzle for nearly an hour. The woman in front of me had this bright yellow umbrella that kept dripping right onto my shoulder. I didn't want to make a fuss, but it was quite annoying. I've never cared much for the color yellow anyway. It washes me out terribly. My mother always told me I was a \"winter\" and should stick to jewel tones. She had this stunning emerald green velvet dress she wore to holiday parties. Velvet is so difficult to care for, though. You can't just toss it in the wash. You have to take it to the dry cleaners, and they use all those harsh chemicals. I read an article in the paper the other day about how those chemicals are getting into the groundwater and affecting the local frogs. It's a shame, really. I used to hear frogs singing every spring from the little creek behind my childhood home. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, losing interest. Yes, I just leave my bag by the door now. I just can't bring myself to go to my classes or book club anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blamed yourself for things more than usual, even when it wasn't really your fault?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, I don't know about that. I don't really think I've been blaming myself for things, no. I mean, I try to just let things be. \n\nLike the other day, I was at the grocery store, trying to find those little tins of anchovies. My father used to love anchovies on his pizza. He’d order a whole pie just for himself with extra cheese and those little salty fish. We used to get them from this Italian place down on Main Street—Luigi's, I think it was called. They had those red and white checkered tablecloths that always felt a bit sticky, no matter how much they wiped them down. I always wondered what they used to clean those tables. Probably some cheap industrial cleaner. You have to be so careful with cleaning products these days. I bought a new bottle of window cleaner last month, and the smell was just overpowering. It smelled like artificial pine needles, but not the nice kind you smell in the forest. I went to a cabin in the mountains once, up near Asheville, and the pine trees there were just magnificent. The air was so crisp. We saw a black bear wandering right near the edge of the woods. It didn't bother us, just sniffed around a fallen log and ambled away. They say you're supposed to make a lot of noise if you see a bear, but I just froze. I couldn't have made a sound if I tried. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling guilty. No, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or blaming myself for things.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had episodes of depression like this before, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for such an extended period?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, this feeling... this low, colorless stretch, it's been with me for about a year and two months now. It just sort of settled in. 

I remember it started right around the time I bought that new winter coat. It was a lovely wool blend, a deep navy blue with these beautiful tortoiseshell buttons. You don't see real tortoiseshell much anymore, it's all plastic now. I suppose that's for the best, for the turtles, I mean. I saw a program on public television about sea turtles hatching on the beach. They have to scramble all the way to the ocean while the seagulls try to swoop down and get them. It's terribly unfair, really. They're just tiny little things, no bigger than a saucer. I had a set of saucers once, part of a tea service my aunt gave me for my wedding. Fine bone china, with little pink roses painted around the edges. I only ever used them when the minister came to call. He was a very tall man, Reverend Higgins. He always had to duck his head when he came through the front door because the frame in that old house was so low. That house had terrible drafts in the winter, no matter how much weather stripping we put around the windows. 

But... oh, what was I saying? Right, the depression. Yes, this is the first time it's lasted this long. A year and two months of this low feeling.

Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, low feeling. It's just like the color has completely gone out of the day before I even manage to get dressed. \n\nSpeaking of getting dressed, I was looking in my closet on Tuesday and realized I haven't worn my yellow cardigan in ages. I bought it at Macy's back when they actually had decent sales and real customer service. There was this lovely woman, Margaret, in the misses department. She always knew exactly what size I needed without even asking. She retired, oh, must be five or six years ago now. Moved down to Florida to live with her oldest daughter, near Tampa, I think. Last week they had this gadget that chops garlic without getting your hands smelly. Because you know, once you get garlic on your fingers, it just stays there for days. They had those red and white checkered tablecloths that always felt a bit sticky, no matter how much they wiped them down. I always wondered what they used to clean those tables. Probably some cheap industrial cleaner. You have to be so careful with cleaning products these days. I bought a new bottle of window cleaner last month, and the smell was just overpowering. It smelled like artificial pine needles, but not the nice kind you smell in the forest. I went to a cabin in the mountains once, up near Asheville, and the pine trees there were just magnificent. The air was so crisp. We saw a black bear wandering right near the edge of the woods. It didn't bother us, just sniffed around a fallen log and ambled away. They say you're supposed to make a lot of noise if you see a bear, but I just froze. I couldn't have made a sound if I tried. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling guilty. No, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or blaming myself for things.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes of depression like this before, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for such an extended period?\n\nPatient: Well, this feeling... this low, colorless stretch, it's been with me for about a year and two months now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before this long stretch? No... no, I don't believe so. I've never really been one to sit around and mope for weeks on end. I always kept busy. 

I used to bake quite a lot, actually. Every Tuesday, I'd make a fresh loaf of sourdough bread. You have to feed the starter, you know, it's almost like having a pet. You keep it in a glass jar on the counter. I kept mine in a big Mason jar. My grandmother used to use those same jars for canning peaches in the summer. We had a peach tree in the backyard when I was a girl. The fruit was so sweet, the juice would just run right down your chin. You had to eat them outside or my mother would scold you for making a sticky mess on the linoleum. She was very particular about her floors. She used this paste wax that smelled faintly of lemon and turpentine. She'd get down on her hands and knees with a rag... people just don't clean like that anymore. Now they have all those fancy steam mops that you just push around. I bought one off the television once, one of those late-night programs. The man on the screen made it look so effortless, gliding it over the tile. But when it arrived, the cord was entirely too short. I had to plug it into an extension cord just to reach the corners of the kitchen, and those extension cords can be such a tripping hazard. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, tripped over a rug and broke her hip last year. It's a terrible thing to break a hip at our age. 

But... what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling down in the past. No, before this year and two months, I never really had periods where I felt this low for weeks at a time.

Yes, I just sit there and the fog rolls in, and I can't seem to figure out what to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Well, moving slowly... yes, I suppose I have been. My body just feels so bogged down lately. It's like it takes me twice as long just to cross the living room or... or button up my sweater. \n\nSpeaking of sweaters, I was trying to button my blue cardigan the other day, the one with the pearl buttons. I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. \n\nBut sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I'll just pace around the house, walking from the kitchen to the hall and back, carrying the same stack of papers in my hand. I don't even know what I'm looking for half the time. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, yes, moving slowly. Yes, it takes me ages to do the simplest things, except for when I'm pacing around with those papers.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — can you pinpoint when these feelings of heaviness and difficulty with daily tasks started?\n\nPatient: Well, when it started... let me think. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. \n\nI remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. \n\nAnyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, or have you been concerned about your appearance or body image?\n\nPatient: Well, my weight... yes, things have definitely changed. What really made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around lately. Some days I'll just completely forget to eat lunch. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. \n\nSpeaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone just wears those elastic belts that stretch out after a month. I bought one for my nephew a few years ago for his birthday, and it was practically useless by Thanksgiving. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. Yes, it's just all turned around. Forgetting lunch, eating crackers... nothing tastes right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, activities... yes, I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to things. I had my watercolor class, the quilting circle, and my Thursday book club. But lately, I'll pack my bag, leave it right there by the front door, and I just... I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out the door. \n\nI used to carry this lovely canvas tote bag to book club. It had a print of Monet's water lilies on it. I bought it at the museum gift shop when they had that Impressionist exhibit back in... oh, it must have been 2015. The line to get in went all the way around the block. We had to stand outside in the drizzle for nearly an hour. The woman in front of me had this bright yellow umbrella that kept dripping right onto my shoulder. I didn't want to make a fuss, but it was quite annoying. I've never cared much for the color yellow anyway. It washes me out terribly. My mother always told me I was a \"winter\" and should stick to jewel tones. She had this stunning emerald green velvet dress she wore to holiday parties. Velvet is so difficult to care for, though. You can't just toss it in the wash. You have to take it to the dry cleaners, and they use all those harsh chemicals. I read an article in the paper the other day about how those chemicals are getting into the groundwater and affecting the local frogs. It's a shame, really. I used to hear frogs singing every spring from the little creek behind my childhood home. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, losing interest. Yes, I just leave my bag by the door now. I just can't bring myself to go to my classes or book club anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blamed yourself for things more than usual, even when it wasn't really your fault?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, I don't know about that. I don't really think I've been blaming myself for things, no. I mean, I try to just let things be. \n\nLike the other day, I was at the grocery store, trying to find those little tins of anchovies. My father used to love anchovies on his pizza. He’d order a whole pie just for himself with extra cheese and those little salty fish. We used to get them from this Italian place down on Main Street—Luigi's, I think it was called. They had those red and white checkered tablecloths that always felt a bit sticky, no matter how much they wiped them down. I always wondered what they used to clean those tables. Probably some cheap industrial cleaner. You have to be so careful with cleaning products these days. I bought a new bottle of window cleaner last month, and the smell was just overpowering. It smelled like artificial pine needles, but not the nice kind you smell in the forest. I went to a cabin in the mountains once, up near Asheville, and the pine trees there were just magnificent. The air was so crisp. We saw a black bear wandering right near the edge of the woods. It didn't bother us, just sniffed around a fallen log and ambled away. They say you're supposed to make a lot of noise if you see a bear, but I just froze. I couldn't have made a sound if I tried. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling guilty. No, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or blaming myself for things.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes of depression like this before, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for such an extended period?\n\nPatient: Well, this feeling... this low, colorless stretch, it's been with me for about a year and two months now. It just sort of settled in. \n\nI remember it started right around the time I bought that new winter coat. It was a lovely wool blend, a deep navy blue with these beautiful tortoiseshell buttons. You don't see real tortoiseshell much anymore, it's all plastic now. I suppose that's for the best, for the turtles, I mean. I saw a program on public television about sea turtles hatching on the beach. They have to scramble all the way to the ocean while the seagulls try to swoop down and get them. It's terribly unfair, really. They're just tiny little things, no bigger than a saucer. I had a set of saucers once, part of a tea service my aunt gave me for my wedding. Fine bone china, with little pink roses painted around the edges. I only ever used them when the minister came to call. He was a very tall man, Reverend Higgins. He always had to duck his head when he came through the front door because the frame in that old house was so low. That house had terrible drafts in the winter, no matter how much weather stripping we put around the windows. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, the depression. Yes, this is the first time it's lasted this long. A year and two months of this low feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in the past where you felt down or lost interest in things for a couple of weeks or more, before this current stretch began?\n\nPatient: Before this long stretch? No... no, I don't believe so. I've never really been one to sit around and mope for weeks on end. I always kept busy. \n\nI used to bake quite a lot, actually. Every Tuesday, I'd make a fresh loaf of sourdough bread. You have to feed the starter, you know, it's almost like having a pet. You keep it in a glass jar on the counter. I kept mine in a big Mason jar. My grandmother used to use those same jars for canning peaches in the summer. We had a peach tree in the backyard when I was a girl. The fruit was so sweet, the juice would just run right down your chin. You had to eat them outside or my mother would scold you for making a sticky mess on the linoleum. She was very particular about her floors. She used this paste wax that smelled faintly of lemon and turpentine. She'd get down on her hands and knees with a rag... people just don't clean like that anymore. Now they have all those fancy steam mops that you just push around. I bought one off the television once, one of those late-night programs. The man on the screen made it look so effortless, gliding it over the tile. But when it arrived, the cord was entirely too short. I had to plug it into an extension cord just to reach the corners of the kitchen, and those extension cords can be such a tripping hazard. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, tripped over a rug and broke her hip last year. It's a terrible thing to break a hip at our age. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling down in the past. No, before this year and two months, I never really had periods where I felt this low for weeks at a time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? About feeling down before this? Yes, I'm quite sure I just said no, I hadn't. Before this past year or so, I never really had those long spells of feeling low. I always just kept moving, kept my hands busy. 

Like I was saying about the baking, keeping busy is so important. If you sit still too long, your joints just stiffen right up. My knees have been giving me such trouble lately, especially when it rains. The barometer drops and I can feel it right in my kneecaps. It's like having a built-in weather station. My uncle Arthur used to say he could predict a thunderstorm better than the man on the local news. He lived out in the country, had a big wraparound porch with a swing. We used to sit out there in the evenings and listen to the cicadas. The noise they make is almost deafening in the late summer, isn't it? Just a constant, buzzing hum. It reminds me of the sound my old refrigerator used to make before it finally gave out on me. I had to throw away a whole gallon of milk and half a roast chicken. It was a shame, really, wasting good food like that. My mother always taught me never to waste food. We'd save bacon grease in a little tin can on the stove to use for frying potatoes. 

But yes, doctor, like I said a moment ago, I really didn't have any long periods of feeling depressed before this current stretch started.

Last week they had this gadget that chops garlic without getting your hands smelly. Because you know, once you get garlic on your fingers, it just stays there for days. No, my energy seems about the same as it usually is for someone my age.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or concentrate on tasks lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Well, focus... yes, it's just been dreadful lately. My body just feels so bogged down lately. It's like it takes me twice as long just to cross the living room or... or button up my sweater. \n\nSpeaking of sweaters, I was trying to button my blue cardigan the other day, the one with the pearl buttons. I got it at a little boutique downtown that closed years ago. They had the most wonderful window displays, always changing them for the seasons. At Christmastime, they’d put up these little mechanical elves that looked like they were actually sewing the garments. It was so charming. You just don't see that kind of effort anymore. Now it's all just big box stores with fluorescent lights that give you a headache if you stay in there too long. I was in one last week trying to find a decent pair of house slippers, and the music they play... it's just awful. Just a constant thumping noise, no melody at all. I don't know how the young people working there can stand it all day. I'd go deaf, or at least go crazy. \n\nBut sometimes, instead of being slow, I get this... this restless feeling. I'll just pace around the house, walking from the kitchen to the hall and back, carrying the same stack of papers in my hand. I don't even know what I'm looking for half the time. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, yes, moving slowly. Yes, it takes me ages to do the simplest things, except for when I'm pacing around with those papers.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — can you pinpoint when these feelings of heaviness and difficulty with daily tasks started?\n\nPatient: Well, when it started... let me think. I suppose this low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months. I know because it was right around the time I decided to have the carpets in the hallway shampooed. I hired this local company, and the young man who came over, he had these big, heavy work boots on. I had to ask him to wear those little blue paper booties over his shoes so he wouldn't track dirt everywhere. Have you seen those? They look exactly like the shower caps they used to give you in hotels. \n\nI remember staying at a Holiday Inn down in Florida years ago, and they had these pink plastic shower caps in a little cardboard box right on the bathroom sink. The water down there was just terrible, though. It smelled exactly like sulfur, like rotten eggs, the absolute minute you turned on the tap. My sister lived in Tampa for a while, and she said you just get used to the smell, but I don't see how anyone could. She had this beautiful grapefruit tree in her backyard, though. The squirrels were an absolute menace to it—they’d take one single bite out of a perfectly good grapefruit and just drop it on the lawn to rot. Such a waste. You can't even buy a decent grapefruit at the grocery store anymore, they're always so dry and pithy inside. \n\nAnyway... what was it you asked? Oh, yes, how long I've felt heavy. Yes, it's been about a year and two months of this colorless stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, or have you been concerned about your appearance or body image?\n\nPatient: Well, my weight... yes, things have definitely changed. What really made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around lately. Some days I'll just completely forget to eat lunch. I'll look up at the clock and it's already four in the afternoon. But then other days, I'll just stand right there at the kitchen counter, eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. \n\nSpeaking of sweets, I used to make this wonderful lemon pound cake. The recipe was my mother's, and she always said the secret was to zest the lemons right before you mix them in, not a minute sooner, or the oils evaporate. I used to buy my lemons from this little produce stand on the corner of Elm and 4th. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these thick suspenders, the kind that buttoned onto the trousers. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone just wears those elastic belts that stretch out after a month. I bought one for my nephew a few years ago for his birthday, and it was practically useless by Thanksgiving. He wore it to dinner, and he kept having to pull his pants up the whole time we were eating. We had turkey that year, but it was terribly dry. I think my sister left it in the oven for an hour too long because she was busy watching some special on television about penguins. They are fascinating creatures, though, aren't they? The way they huddle together in the freezing cold to stay warm. I saw a documentary where they showed them walking for miles across the ice just to find food for their babies. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight and eating. Yes, it's just all turned around. Forgetting lunch, eating crackers... nothing tastes right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, activities... yes, I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to things. I had my watercolor class, the quilting circle, and my Thursday book club. But lately, I'll pack my bag, leave it right there by the front door, and I just... I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out the door. \n\nI used to carry this lovely canvas tote bag to book club. It had a print of Monet's water lilies on it. I bought it at the museum gift shop when they had that Impressionist exhibit back in... oh, it must have been 2015. The line to get in went all the way around the block. We had to stand outside in the drizzle for nearly an hour. The woman in front of me had this bright yellow umbrella that kept dripping right onto my shoulder. I didn't want to make a fuss, but it was quite annoying. I've never cared much for the color yellow anyway. It washes me out terribly. My mother always told me I was a \"winter\" and should stick to jewel tones. She had this stunning emerald green velvet dress she wore to holiday parties. Velvet is so difficult to care for, though. You can't just toss it in the wash. You have to take it to the dry cleaners, and they use all those harsh chemicals. I read an article in the paper the other day about how those chemicals are getting into the groundwater and affecting the local frogs. It's a shame, really. I used to hear frogs singing every spring from the little creek behind my childhood home. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, losing interest. Yes, I just leave my bag by the door now. I just can't bring myself to go to my classes or book club anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blamed yourself for things more than usual, even when it wasn't really your fault?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, I don't know about that. I don't really think I've been blaming myself for things, no. I mean, I try to just let things be. \n\nLike the other day, I was at the grocery store, trying to find those little tins of anchovies. My father used to love anchovies on his pizza. He’d order a whole pie just for himself with extra cheese and those little salty fish. We used to get them from this Italian place down on Main Street—Luigi's, I think it was called. They had those red and white checkered tablecloths that always felt a bit sticky, no matter how much they wiped them down. I always wondered what they used to clean those tables. Probably some cheap industrial cleaner. You have to be so careful with cleaning products these days. I bought a new bottle of window cleaner last month, and the smell was just overpowering. It smelled like artificial pine needles, but not the nice kind you smell in the forest. I went to a cabin in the mountains once, up near Asheville, and the pine trees there were just magnificent. The air was so crisp. We saw a black bear wandering right near the edge of the woods. It didn't bother us, just sniffed around a fallen log and ambled away. They say you're supposed to make a lot of noise if you see a bear, but I just froze. I couldn't have made a sound if I tried. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling guilty. No, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or blaming myself for things.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes of depression like this before, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for such an extended period?\n\nPatient: Well, this feeling... this low, colorless stretch, it's been with me for about a year and two months now. It just sort of settled in. \n\nI remember it started right around the time I bought that new winter coat. It was a lovely wool blend, a deep navy blue with these beautiful tortoiseshell buttons. You don't see real tortoiseshell much anymore, it's all plastic now. I suppose that's for the best, for the turtles, I mean. I saw a program on public television about sea turtles hatching on the beach. They have to scramble all the way to the ocean while the seagulls try to swoop down and get them. It's terribly unfair, really. They're just tiny little things, no bigger than a saucer. I had a set of saucers once, part of a tea service my aunt gave me for my wedding. Fine bone china, with little pink roses painted around the edges. I only ever used them when the minister came to call. He was a very tall man, Reverend Higgins. He always had to duck his head when he came through the front door because the frame in that old house was so low. That house had terrible drafts in the winter, no matter how much weather stripping we put around the windows. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, the depression. Yes, this is the first time it's lasted this long. A year and two months of this low feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in the past where you felt down or lost interest in things for a couple of weeks or more, before this current stretch began?\n\nPatient: Before this long stretch? No... no, I don't believe so. I've never really been one to sit around and mope for weeks on end. I always kept busy. \n\nI used to bake quite a lot, actually. Every Tuesday, I'd make a fresh loaf of sourdough bread. You have to feed the starter, you know, it's almost like having a pet. You keep it in a glass jar on the counter. I kept mine in a big Mason jar. My grandmother used to use those same jars for canning peaches in the summer. We had a peach tree in the backyard when I was a girl. The fruit was so sweet, the juice would just run right down your chin. You had to eat them outside or my mother would scold you for making a sticky mess on the linoleum. She was very particular about her floors. She used this paste wax that smelled faintly of lemon and turpentine. She'd get down on her hands and knees with a rag... people just don't clean like that anymore. Now they have all those fancy steam mops that you just push around. I bought one off the television once, one of those late-night programs. The man on the screen made it look so effortless, gliding it over the tile. But when it arrived, the cord was entirely too short. I had to plug it into an extension cord just to reach the corners of the kitchen, and those extension cords can be such a tripping hazard. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, tripped over a rug and broke her hip last year. It's a terrible thing to break a hip at our age. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling down in the past. No, before this year and two months, I never really had periods where I felt this low for weeks at a time.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch of feeling down, have you ever had any other episodes where you felt depressed or lost interest in things for a couple of weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? I always just kept moving, kept my hands busy. \n\nLike I was saying about the baking, keeping busy is so important. If you sit still too long, your joints just stiffen right up. My uncle Arthur used to say he could predict a thunderstorm better than the man on the local news. He lived out in the country, had a big wraparound porch with a swing. We used to sit out there in the evenings and listen to the cicadas. The noise they make is almost deafening in the late summer, isn't it? Just a constant, buzzing hum. It reminds me of the sound my old refrigerator used to make before it finally gave out on me. I had to throw away a whole gallon of milk and half a roast chicken. It was a shame, really, wasting good food like that. My mother always taught me never to waste food. We'd save bacon grease in a little tin can on the stove to use for frying potatoes. \n\nBut yes, doctor, like I said a moment ago, I really didn't have any long periods of feeling depressed before this current stretch started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
